   [191][440px-Orders_of_magnitude_%28english_annotations%29.png] Objects
   of sizes in different order of magnitude (at inconsistent
   intervals)[192][400px-Scales_of_size.jpg] Graphical overview of sizes

   The following are examples of [193]orders of magnitude for different
   [194]lengths.

Overview

   [[195]edit]
   Scale Range ([196]m) Unit Example items
   ≥ <
   [197]Subatomic – 0 – [198]Gravitational singularity
   10^−36 10^−33 [199]ℓ[P] Fixed value (not a range). [200]Quantum foam,
   [201]string
   10^−18 10^−15 [202]am [203]Proton, [204]neutron, [205]pion
   [206]Atomic to cellular [207]10^−15 10^−12 [208]fm [209]Atomic nucleus
   [210]10^−12 10^−9 [211]pm [212]Wavelength of [213]gamma rays and
   [214]X-rays, [215]hydrogen atom
   [216]10^−9 10^−6 [217]nm [218]DNA [219]helix, [220]virus, wavelength of
   [221]optical spectrum, transistors used in CPUs
   [222]Cellular to human [223]10^−6 10^−3 [224]μm [225]Bacterium,
   [226]fog water droplet, human [227]hair's diameter^[228][note 1]
   [229]10^−3 1 [230]mm [231]Mosquito, [232]golf ball, [233]domestic cat,
   [234]violin, [235]football
   [236]Human to astronomical 1 10^3 [237]m [238]Piano, [239]human,
   [240]automobile, [241]sperm whale, [242]football field, [243]Eiffel
   Tower
   [244]10^3 10^6 [245]km [246]Mount Everest, length of [247]Panama Canal

Subatomic scale

   [[281]edit]
   Factor ([282]m) Multiple Value Item
   0 0 0 [283]Singularity
   10^−35 1 [284]Planck length 0.0000162 qm  [285]Planck length; typical
   scale of hypothetical [286]loop quantum gravity or size of a
   hypothetical [287]string and of [288]branes; according to [289]string
   theory, lengths smaller than this do not make any [290]physical
   sense.^[291][1] [292]Quantum foam is thought to exist at this scale.
   10^−24 [293]1 yoctometer ([294]ym) 142 ym Effective [295]cross section
   radius of 1 [296]MeV [297]neutrinos^[298][2]
   10^−21 [299]1 zeptometer ([300]zm) [301]Preons, hypothetical particles
   proposed as subcomponents of quarks and leptons; the upper bound for
   the width of a [302]cosmic string in string theory
   7 zm Effective cross section radius of high-energy
   [303]neutrinos^[304][3]
   310 zm [305]De Broglie wavelength of [306]protons at the [307]Large
   Hadron Collider (4 TeV as of 2012^[308][update])
   10^−18 [309]1 attometer ([310]am) Upper limit for the size of
   [311]quarks and [312]electrons
   Sensitivity of the [313]LIGO detector for [314]gravitational
   waves^[315][4]
   Upper bound of the typical size range for "fundamental
   [316]strings"^[317][1]
   10^−17 [318]10 am Range of the [319]weak force
   10^−16 [320]100 am 850 am Approximate proton radius^[321][5]

Atomic to cellular scale

   [[322]edit]
   Factor ([323]m) Multiple Value Item
   10^−15 [324]1 femtometer ([325]fm, fermi) 1 fm Approximate limit of the
   [326]gluon-mediated [327]color force between
   [328]quarks^[329][6]^[330][7]
   1.5 fm Effective cross section radius of an 11 MeV [331]proton^[332][8]
   2.81794 fm [333]Classical electron radius^[334][9]
   3 fm Approximate limit of the [335]meson-mediated [336]nuclear binding
   force^[337][6]^[338][7]
   750 to 822.25 fm Longest [339]wavelength of [340]gamma rays
   10^−12 [341]1 picometer ([342]pm) 1.75 to 15 fm Diameter range of the
   [343]atomic nucleus^[344][1]^[345][10]
   1 pm Distance between [346]atomic nuclei in a [347]white dwarf
   2.4 pm [348]Compton wavelength of [349]electron
   5 pm Wavelength of shortest [350]X-rays
   10^−11 [351]10 pm 28 pm Radius of [352]helium atom
   53 pm [353]Bohr radius (radius of a [354]hydrogen atom)
   10^−10 [355]100 pm 100 pm 1 [356]ångström (also [357]covalent radius of
   [358]sulfur atom^[359][11])
   154 pm Length of a typical [360]covalent bond ([361]C–C)
   280 pm Average size of the [362]water molecule (actual lengths may
   vary)
   500 pm Width of [363]protein [364]α helix
   10^−9 [365]1 nanometer ([366]nm) 1 nm Diameter of a [367]carbon
   nanotube^[368][12]

   Diameter of smallest transistor gate (as of 2016)^[369][13]
   2 nm Diameter of the [370]DNA helix^[371][14]
   2.5 nm Smallest [372]microprocessor [373]transistor [374]gate oxide
   thickness (as of January 2007^[375][update])^[[376]citation needed]
   3.4 nm Length of a [377]DNA [378]turn (10 [379]bp)^[380][15]
   6–10 nm Thickness of [381]cell membrane
   10^−8 [382]10 nm 10 nm Upper range of thickness of [383]cell wall in
   [384]Gram-negative [385]bacteria^[386][16]
   10 nm As of 2016^[387][update], the [388]10 nanometer was the smallest
   [389]semiconductor device fabrication [390]node^[391][17]
   40 nm Extreme [392]ultraviolet wavelength
   50 nm [393]Flying height of the [394]head of a [395]hard disk^[396][18]
   10^−7 [397]100 nm 121.6 nm Wavelength of the [398]Lyman-alpha
   line^[399][19]
   120 nm Typical diameter of the [400]human immunodeficiency virus
   (HIV)^[401][20]
   400–700 nm Approximate wavelength range of [402]visible light^[403][21]

Cellular to human scale

   [[404]edit]
   Factor ([405]m) Multiple Value Item
   10^−6 [406]1 micrometer ([407]μm)

   (also called 1 micron)
   1–4 μm Typical length of a bacterium^[408][22]
   4 μm Typical diameter of [409]spider silk^[410][23]
   7 μm Typical size of a [411]red blood cell^[412][24]
   10^−5 [413]10 μm 10 μm Typical size of a fog, mist, or cloud water
   droplet
   10 μm Width of [414]transistors in the [415]Intel 4004, the world's
   first commercial [416]microprocessor
   12 μm Width of [417]acrylic fiber
   17-181 μm Width range of human hair^[418][25]
   10^−4 [419]100 μm 340 μm Size of a [420]pixel on a 17-inch monitor with
   a resolution of 1024×768
   560 μm Thickness of the central area of a human [421]cornea^[422][26]
   750 μm Maximum diameter of [423]Thiomargarita namibiensis, the second
   largest bacterium ever discovered
   10^−3 [424]1 millimeter ([425]mm) ~5 mm Length of an average [426]flea
   is 1–10 mm (usually <5 mm)^[427][27]
   2.54 mm One-tenth inch; distance between pins in [428]DIP
   (dual-inline-package) electronic components
   5.70 mm Diameter of the projectile in [429]5.56×45mm NATO ammunition
   10^−2 [430]1 centimeter ([431]cm) 20 mm Approximate width of an adult
   human [432]finger
   54 mm × 86 mm Dimensions of a [433]credit card, according to the
   [434]ISO/IEC 7810 ID-1 standard
   73–75 mm Diameter of a [435]baseball, according to [436]Major League
   Baseball guidelines^[437][28]
   10^−1 [438]1 decimeter ([439]dm) 120 mm Diameter of a [440]compact disc
   660 mm Length of the longest [441]pine cones, produced by the
   [442]sugar pine^[443][29]
   900 mm Average length of a [444]rapier, a [445]fencing sword^[446][30]

Human to astronomical scale

   [[447]edit]
   [448][220px-Size_planets_comparison.jpg] Planets of the Solar System to
   scale
   Factor ([449]m) Multiple Value Item
   1 (10^0) [450]1 meter ([451]m) 1 m (exactly) Since 2019, defined as the
   length of the path travelled by [452]light in a vacuum during a time
   interval of 1/299,792,458 of a [453]second, where the second is defined
   by a hyperfine transition frequency of [454]caesium.
   2.72 m Height of [455]Robert Wadlow, tallest-known human.^[456][31]
   8.38 m Length of a London bus ([457]AEC Routemaster)
   10^1 [458]1 decameter ([459]dam) 33 m Length of the longest-known
   [460]blue whale^[461][32]
   52 m Height of the [462]Niagara Falls^[463][33]
   93.47 m Height of the [464]Statue of Liberty
   10^2 [465]1 hectometer ([466]hm) 105 m Length of a typical
   [467]football field
   137 m (147 m) Height (present and original) of the [468]Great Pyramid
   of Giza
   300 m Height of the [469]Eiffel Tower, one of the famous monuments of
   Paris
   979 m Height of the [470]Salto Angel, the world's highest free-falling
   waterfall ([471]Venezuela)
   10^3 [472]1 kilometer ([473]km) 2.3 km Length of the [474]Three Gorges
   Dam, the largest [475]dam in the world^[476][34]^[477][35]
   3.1 km Narrowest width of the [478]Strait of Messina, separating
   [479]Italy and [480]Sicily
   8.848 km Height of [481]Mount Everest, the highest mountain on Earth
   10^4 [482]10 km 10.9 km Depth of the [483]Challenger Deep in the
   [484]Mariana Trench, the deepest-known point on Earth's surface
   27 km Circumference of the [485]Large Hadron Collider, as of May
   2010^[486][update] the largest and highest energy [487]particle
   accelerator
   42.195 km Length of a [488]marathon
   10^5 [489]100 km 100 km The distance the [490]IAU considers to be the
   limit to [491]space, called the [492]Karman line
   163 km Length of the [493]Suez Canal, connecting the [494]Mediterranean
   Sea to the [495]Red Sea
   491 km Length of the [496]Pyrenees, the [497]mountain range separating
   [498]Spain and [499]France
   600km [500]Thermosphere height
   974.6 km Greatest diameter of the dwarf planet [501]Ceres.^[502][36]
   10^6 [503]1 megameter ([504]Mm) 2.38 Mm Diameter of dwarf planet
   [505]Pluto, formerly the smallest [506]planet category^[507][note 2] in
   the Solar System
   3.48 Mm Diameter of the [508]Moon
   5.2 Mm Typical distance covered by the winner of the [509]24 Hours of
   Le Mans automobile endurance race
   6.259 Mm Length of the [510]Great Wall of China
   6.371 Mm Average [511]radius of Earth
   6.378 Mm Equatorial radius of Earth
   6.6 Mm Approximate length of the two longest rivers, the [512]Nile and
   the [513]Amazon
   7.821 Mm Length of the [514]Trans-Canada Highway
   9.288 Mm Length of the [515]Trans-Siberian Railway, longest in the
   world

Astronomical scale

   [[516]edit]
   Factor ([517]m) Multiple Value Item
   10^7 [518]10 Mm 12.756 Mm Equatorial diameter of Earth
   20.004 Mm Length of a [519]meridian on Earth (distance between Earth's
   poles along the surface)^[520][37]
   40.075 Mm Length of Earth's [521]equator
   10^8 [522]100 Mm 142.984 Mm Diameter of [523]Jupiter
   299.792 Mm Distance traveled by light in vacuum in one second (a
   [524]light-second, exactly 299,792,458 m by definition of the
   [525]speed of light)
   384.4 Mm [526]Moon's orbital distance from Earth
   10^9 [527]1 gigameter ([528]Gm) 1.39 Gm Diameter of the [529]Sun
   5.15 Gm Greatest mileage ever recorded by a car (3.2 million miles by a
   1966 [530]Volvo P-1800S)^[531][38]
   10^10 [532]10 Gm 18 Gm Approximately one [533]light-minute
   10^11 [534]100 Gm 150 Gm 1 [535]astronomical unit (au); mean distance
   between Earth and Sun
   10^12 [536]1 terameter (Tm) 1.3 Tm Optical diameter of [537]Betelgeuse
   1.4 Tm Orbital distance of [538]Saturn from Sun
   2 Tm Estimated optical diameter of [539]VY Canis Majoris, one of the
   [540]largest-known stars
   5.9 Tm Orbital distance of Pluto from the Sun
   ~ 7.5 Tm Outer boundary of the [541]Kuiper belt
   10^13 [542]10 Tm Diameter of the [543]Solar System as a whole^[544][1]
   16.09 Tm Total length of DNA molecules in all cells of an adult human
   body^[545][39]
   21.49 Tm Distance of the [546]Voyager 1 spacecraft from Sun (as of Oct
   2018^[547][update]), the farthest man-made object so far^[548][40]
   62.03 Tm Estimated radius of the [549]event horizon of the
   [550]supermassive black hole in [551]NGC 4889, the largest-known
   [552]black hole to date
   10^14 [553]100 Tm 180 Tm Size of the [554]debris disk around the star
   [555]51 Pegasi^[556][41]
   10^15 [557]1 petameter (Pm) ~7.5 Pm Supposed outer boundary of the
   [558]Oort cloud (~ 50,000 au)
   9.461 Pm Distance traveled by light in vacuum in one year; at its
   current speed, Voyager 1 would need 17,500 years to travel this
   distance
   10^16 [559]10 Pm 30.857 Pm 1 [560]parsec
   39.9 Pm Distance to nearest star ([561]Proxima Centauri)
   41.3 Pm As of March 2013, distance to nearest discovered
   [562]extrasolar planet ([563]Alpha Centauri Bc)
   10^17 [564]100 Pm 193 Pm As of October 2010, distance to nearest
   discovered extrasolar planet with potential to support life as
   presently defined by science ([565]Gliese 581 d)
   615 Pm Approximate radius of humanity's radio bubble, caused by
   high-power TV broadcasts leaking through the atmosphere into outer
   space
   10^18 [566]1 exameter (Em) 1.9 Em Distance to nearby [567]solar twin
   ([568]HIP 56948), a star with properties virtually identical to the
   Sun^[569][42]
   10^19 [570]10 Em 9.46 Em Average thickness of [571]Milky Way
   Galaxy^[572][43] (1,000 to 3,000 ly by [573]21 cm
   observations^[574][44])
   10^20 [575]100 Em 113.5 Em Thickness of [576]Milky Way Galaxy's gaseous
   disk^[577][45]
   10^21 [578]1 zettameter (Zm)
   1.54 Zm Distance to [579]SN 1987A, the most recent naked eye supernova
   1.62 Zm Distance to the [580]Large Magellanic Cloud (a [581]dwarf
   galaxy [582]orbiting the [583]Milky Way)
   1.66 Zm Distance to the [584]Small Magellanic Cloud (another dwarf
   galaxy orbiting the Milky Way)
   1.9 Zm Diameter of galactic disk of [585]Milky Way
   Galaxy^[586][46]^[587][47]^[588][48]^[589][49]
   6.15 Zm Diameter of the low surface brightness disc halo of the giant
   spiral galaxy [590]Malin 1
   10^22 [591]10 Zm 13.25 Zm Radius of the diffuse stellar halo of [592]IC
   1101, one of the largest-known galaxies
   24 Zm Distance to [593]Andromeda Galaxy
   30.857 Zm 1 [594]megaparsec
   50 Zm Diameter of [595]Local Group of [596]galaxies
   10^23 [597]100 Zm 300–600 Zm Distance to [598]Virgo cluster of
   [599]galaxies
   10^24 [600]1 yottameter (Ym) 2.19 Ym Diameter of the [601]Local
   Supercluster and the largest voids and filaments
   2.8 Ym [602]End of Greatness
   ~5 Ym Diameter of the [603]Horologium Supercluster^[604][50]
   9.461 Ym Diameter of the [605]Pisces–Cetus Supercluster Complex, the
   supercluster complex which includes Earth
   10^25 [606]10 Ym 13 Ym Length of the [607]Sloan Great Wall, a giant
   wall of galaxies ([608]galactic filament)^[609][51]
   30.857 Ym 1 [610]gigaparsec
   37.84 Ym Length of the [611]Huge-LQG, a group of 73 [612]quasars
   10^26 [613]100 Ym 95 Ym Estimated [614]light travel distance to certain
   [615]quasars. Length of the [616]Hercules–Corona Borealis Great Wall, a
   colossal wall of galaxies, the largest and the most massive structure
   in the observable universe as of 2014
   127 Ym Estimated light travel distance to [617]GN-z11, the [618]most
   distant object ever observed
   870 Ym Approximate diameter ([619]comoving distance) of the
   [620]visible universe^[621][1]
   10^27 [622]1 Rm 1.2 Rm Lower bound of the (possibly infinite) radius of
   the universe, if it is a [623]3-sphere, according to one estimate using
   the [624]WMAP data at 95% confidence^[625][52] It equivalently implies
   that there are at minimum 21 [626]particle horizon-sized volumes in the
   universe.

1 quectometer and less

   [[634]edit]

   The quectometre
   ([635]SI symbol: qm) is a [636]unit of [637]length in the [638]metric
   system equal to 10^−30 [639]metres. To help compare different
   [640]orders of magnitude, this section lists [641]lengths shorter than
   10^−30 [642]m (1 qm).
     * 1.6 × 10^−5 quectometers (1.6 × 10^−35 meters) – the [643]Planck
       length (Measures of distance shorter than this do not make physical
       sense, according to current theories of [644]physics.)
     * 1 qm – 1 quectometer, the smallest named subdivision of the meter
       in the [645]SI base unit of length, one nonillionth of a
       meter.^[646][56]

1 rontometer

   [[647]edit]

   The rontometre
   ([648]SI symbol: rm) is a [649]unit of [650]length in the [651]metric
   system equal to 10^−27 [652]metres.
     * 1 rm – 1 rontometer, a subdivision of the meter in the [653]SI base
       unit of length, one octillionth of a meter.^[654][56]

10 rontometers

   [[655]edit]
     * 10 rm – the length of one side of a square whose area is one
       [656]shed, a unit of [657]target cross section used in [658]nuclear
       physics

1 yoctometer

   [[659]edit]

   The yoctometre
   ([660]SI symbol: ym) is a [661]unit of [662]length in the [663]metric
   system equal to 10^−24 [664]metres.
     * 2 ym – the effective cross-section radius of 1 [665]MeV
       [666]neutrinos as measured by [667]Clyde Cowan and [668]Frederick
       Reines.^[669][57]

1 zeptometer

   [[670]edit]

   The zeptometre
   ([671]SI symbol: zm) is a [672]unit of [673]length in the [674]metric
   system equal to 10^−21 [675]metres. To help compare different
   [676]orders of magnitude, this section lists [677]lengths between
   10^−21 [678]m and 10^−20 m (1 zm and 10 zm).
     * 2 zm – the upper bound for the width of a [679]cosmic string in
       string theory.^[[680]citation needed]
     * 2 zm – radius of effective [681]cross section for a [682]20
       [683]GeV [684]neutrino scattering off a [685]nucleon^[686][3]
     * 7 zm – radius of effective cross section for a
       [687]250^[[688]broken anchor] GeV neutrino scattering off a
       nucleon^[689][3]

10 zeptometers

   [[690]edit]

   To help compare different [691]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [692]lengths between 10^−20 [693]m and 10^−19 m (10 [694]zm and 100
   zm).

100 zeptometers

   [[695]edit]

   To help compare different [696]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   lengths between 10^−19 [697]m and 10^−18 m (100 [698]zm and 1 [699]am).
     * 177 zm – [700]de Broglie wavelength of [701]protons at the
       [702]Large Hadron Collider (7 TeV as of 2010)^[[703]citation
       needed]

1 attometer

   [[704]edit]

   The attometre
   ([705]SI symbol: am) is a [706]unit of [707]length in the [708]metric
   system equal to 10^−18 [709]metres. To help compare different
   [710]orders of magnitude, this section lists [711]lengths between
   10^−18 [712]m and 10^−17 m (1 am and 10 am).
     * 1 am – sensitivity of the [713]LIGO detector for [714]gravitational
       waves^[[715]citation needed]
     * 1 am – upper limit for the size of [716]quarks and
       [717]electrons^[[718]citation needed]

10 attometers

   [[719]edit]

   To help compare different [720]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [721]lengths between 10^−17 [722]m and 10^−16 m (10 [723]am and 100
   am).
     * 10–100 am – range of the weak force^[724][58]
     * 86 am – charge radius of a [725]Bottom eta meson^[726][59]

100 attometers

   [[727]edit]

   To help compare different [728]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   lengths between 10^−16 [729]m and 10^−15 m (100 [730]am and 1 [731]fm).
     * 831 am – approximate proton radius^[732][60]^[733][61]

1 femtometer (or 1 fermi)

   [[734]edit]

   The [735]femtometre
   ([736]SI symbol: fm) is a [737]unit of [738]length in the [739]metric
   system equal to 10^−15 [740]metres. In [741]particle physics, this unit
   is sometimes called a fermi, also with abbreviation "fm". To help
   compare different [742]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [743]lengths between 10^−15 [744]meters and 10^−14 meters (1 femtometer
   and 10 fm).
     * 1 fm – diameter of a [745]neutron, approximate range-limit of the
       [746]color force carried between [747]quarks by
       [748]gluons^[749][6]^[750][7]
     * 1.5 fm – diameter of the [751]scattering cross section of an 11
       [752]MeV [753]proton with a target proton^[754][8]
     * 1.75 fm – the effective charge diameter of a [755]proton^[756][62]
     * 2.81794 fm – [757]classical electron radius^[758][63]
     * 3 fm – approximate range-limit of the [759]nuclear binding force
       mediated by [760]mesons^[761][6]^[762][7]
     * 7 fm – the radius of the effective scattering cross section for a
       gold nucleus scattering a [763]6^[[764]broken anchor] [765]MeV
       [766]alpha particle over 140 degrees^[767][8]

10 femtometers

   [[768]edit]

   To help compare different [769]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [770]lengths between 10^−14 [771]m and 10^−13 m (10 [772]fm and 100
   fm).
     * 1.75 to 15 fm – diameter range of the [773]atomic
       nucleus^[[774]citation needed]
     * 10 fm – the length of one side of a square whose area is one
       [775]barn (10^−28 m^2), a unit of [776]target cross section used in
       [777]nuclear physics
     * 30.8568 fm – 1 quectoparsec (10^−30 [778]parsecs)

100 femtometers

   [[779]edit]

   To help compare different [780]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   lengths between 10^−13 [781]m and 10^−12 m (100 [782]fm and 1 [783]pm).
     * 570 fm – typical distance from the atomic nucleus of the two
       innermost electrons (electrons in the 1s shell) in the [784]uranium
       atom, the heaviest naturally-occurring atom

1 picometer

   [[785]edit]

   The [786]picometre
   ([787]SI symbol: pm) is a [788]unit of [789]length in the [790]metric
   system equal to 10^−12 [791]metres
   (⁠1/1000000000000⁠ m = 0.000000000001 m). To help compare different
   [792]orders of magnitude this section lists [793]lengths between 10^−12
   and 10^−11 m (1 pm and 10 pm).
     * 1 pm – distance between [794]atomic nuclei in a [795]white
       dwarf^[[796]citation needed]
     * 1 pm – reference value of particle displacement in
       acoustics^[797][64]
     * 2.4 pm – the [798]Compton wavelength of an [799]electron
     * 5 pm – shorter [800]X-ray wavelengths (approx.)

10 picometers

   [[801]edit]

   To help compare different [802]orders of magnitude this section lists
   [803]lengths between 10^−11 and 10^−10 [804]m (10 pm and 100 pm).
     * 25 pm – approximate radius of a helium atom, the smallest neutral
       atom^[805][65]^[806][66]
     * 30.8568 pm – 1 rontoparsec
     * 50 pm – radius of a hydrogen atom
     * 50 pm – [807]bohr radius: approximate radius of a hydrogen atom
     * ~50 pm – best resolution of a [808]high-resolution transmission
       electron microscope
     * 60 pm – radius of a carbon atom
     * 93 pm – length of a [809]diatomic carbon molecule
     * 96 pm – H–O bond length in a [810]water molecule

   [813]lengths between 10^−10 and 10^−9 [814]m (100 pm and 1 nm; 1 [815]Å
   and 10 Å).
     * 100 pm – 1 [816]ångström
     * 100 pm – [817]covalent radius of [818]sulfur atom^[819][67]
     * 120 pm – [820]van der Waals radius of a neutral [821]hydrogen

     * 120 pm – radius of a gold atom
     * 126 pm – covalent radius of [823]ruthenium atom
     * 135 pm – covalent radius of [824]technetium atom
     * 150 pm – length of a typical [825]covalent bond ([826]C–C)
     * 153 pm – covalent radius of [827]silver atom
     * 155 pm – covalent radius of [828]zirconium atom
     * 175 pm – covalent radius of [829]thulium atom
     * 200 pm – highest resolution of a typical [830]electron
       microscope^[831][69]
     * 225 pm – covalent radius of [832]caesium atom
     * 280 pm – average size of the [833]water molecule
     * 298 pm – radius of a [834]caesium atom, [835]calculated to be the
       largest atomic radius
     * 340 pm – thickness of single layer [836]graphene
     * 356.68 pm – width of [837]diamond [838]unit cell
     * 403 pm – width of [839]lithium fluoride unit cell
     * 500 pm – Width of [840]protein [841]α helix
     * 543 pm – silicon lattice spacing
     * 560 pm – width of [842]sodium chloride unit cell
     * 700 pm – width of [843]glucose molecule

     * 780 pm – mean width of [846]quartz unit cell
     * 820 pm – mean width of [847]ice unit cell
     * 900 pm – mean width of [848]coesite unit cell

1 nanometer

   [[849]edit]

   The [850]nanometre
   ([851]SI symbol: nm) is a [852]unit of [853]length in the [854]metric
   system equal to 10^−9 [855]metres (⁠1/1000000000⁠ m = 0.000000001 m).
   To help compare different [856]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   lengths between 10^−9 and 10^−8 m (1 nm and 10 nm).
     * 1 nm – diameter of a [857]carbon nanotube
     * 1 nm – roughly the length of a [858]sucrose molecule, calculated by
       [859]Albert Einstein
     * 2.3 nm – length of a [860]phospholipid
     * 2.3 nm – smallest [861]gate oxide thickness in [862]microprocessors
     * 3 nm – width of a [863]DNA helix
     * 3 nm – [864]flying height of the [865]head of a [866]hard disk
     * 3 nm – the [867]average half-pitch of a memory cell manufactured
       circa 2022
     * 3.4 nm – length of a DNA [868]turn (10 [869]bp)
     * 3.8 nm – size of an [870]albumin molecule
     * 5 nm – size of the gate length of a 16 nm processor
     * 5 nm – the [871]average half-pitch of a memory cell manufactured
       circa 2019–2020
     * 6 nm – length of a [872]phospholipid bilayer
     * 6–10 nm – thickness of cell membrane
     * 6.8 nm – width of a [873]haemoglobin molecule
     * 7 nm – diameter of [874]actin filaments
     * 7 nm – the [875]average half-pitch of a memory cell manufactured
       circa 2018
     * 10 nm – thickness of [876]cell wall in [877]Gram-negative
       [878]bacteria

       circa 2016 [892]–2017

     * 18 nm – diameter of [896]tobacco mosaic virus^[897][74] (Generally,
       [898]viruses range in size from 20 nm to 450 nm.)^[[899]citation

     * 20 nm – thickness of [904]bacterial [905]flagellum^[906][76]

     * 25 nm – diameter of a [910]microtubule
     * 30 nm – lower size of cooking oil smoke
     * 30.8568 nm – 1 yoctoparsec
     * 32 nm – the [911]average half-pitch of a memory cell manufactured
       circa 2009–2010
     * 40 nm – extreme [912]ultraviolet wavelength
     * 45 nm – the [913]average half-pitch of a memory cell manufactured
       circa 2007–2008
     * 50 nm – upper size for airborne [914]virus particles
     * 50 nm – [915]flying height of the [916]head of a [917]hard

     * 65 nm – the [919]average half-pitch of a memory cell manufactured

     * 90 nm – [921]human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) (generally,

     * 90 nm – the [923]average half-pitch of a memory cell manufactured

     * 100 nm – Length of a [924]mesoporous silica nanoparticle

100 nanometers

   [[925]edit]
   [926][330px-Comparison_semiconductor_process_nodes.svg.png] Comparison
   of sizes of semiconductor manufacturing process nodes with some
   microscopic objects and visible light wavelengths. At this scale, the
   [927]width of a human hair is about 10 times that of the
   image.^[928][79]

   To help compare different [929]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [930]lengths between 10^−7 and 10^−6 [931]m (100 [932]nm and
   1 [933]μm).
     * 100 nm – greatest particle size that can fit through a
       [934]surgical mask^[935][80]
     * 100 nm – 90% of particles in wood [936]smoke are smaller than
       this.^[[937]citation needed]
     * 120 nm – greatest particle size that can fit through a [938]ULPA
       filter^[939][81]
     * 120 nm – diameter of a [940]human immunodeficiency virus
       (HIV)^[941][82]
     * 120 nm – approximate diameter of [942]SARS-CoV-2^[943][83]
     * 125 nm – standard depth of pits on [944]compact discs (width:
       500 nm, length: 850 nm to 3.5 μm)
     * 180 nm – typical length of the [945]rabies virus
     * 200 nm – typical size of a [946]Mycoplasma bacterium, among the
       smallest bacteria
     * 300 nm – greatest particle size that can fit through a [947]HEPA
       (high efficiency particulate air) filter (N100 removes up to 99.97%
       at 300 nm, N95 removes up to 95% at 300 nm)^[948][84]
     * 300–400 nm – near [949]ultraviolet wavelength
     * 400–420 nm – wavelength of [950]violet light (see [951]Color and
       [952]Visible spectrum)
     * 420–440 nm – wavelength of [953]indigo light
     * 440–500 nm – wavelength of [954]blue light
     * 500–520 nm – wavelength of [955]cyan light
     * 520–565 nm – wavelength of [956]green light
     * 565–590 nm – wavelength of [957]yellow light
     * 590–625 nm – wavelength of [958]orange light
     * 625–700 nm – wavelength of [959]red light
     * 700–1.4 μm – wavelength of [960]near-infrared radiation

1 micrometer (or 1 micron)

   [[961]edit]
   [962][170px-Loxoceles_reclusa_iconized_thread.png] The silk for a
   spider's web is 5–7 μm (0.00020–0.00028 in) wide.

   The [963]micrometre
   ([964]SI symbol: μm) is a [965]unit of [966]length in the [967]metric
   system equal to 10^−6 [968]metres (⁠1/1000000⁠ m = 0.000001 m). To help
   compare different [969]orders of magnitude, this section lists some
   items with lengths between 10^−6 and 10^−5 m (between 1 and 10
   [970]micrometers, or μm).
     * ~0.7–300 μm – wavelength of [971]infrared radiation
     * 1 μm – the side of a [972]square of area [973]10^−12 m^2
     * 1 μm – edge of [974]cube of volume [975]10^−18 m^3 (1 fL)
     * 1–10 μm – diameter of a typical [976]bacterium^[[977]citation
       needed]
     * 1 μm – length of a [978]lysosome
     * 1–2 μm – [979]anthrax spore^[980][85]
     * 2 μm – length of an average [981]E. coli bacteria
     * 3–4 μm – size of a typical [982]yeast cell^[983][86]
     * 5 μm – length of a typical human [984]spermatozoon's head^[985][87]
     * 6 μm – thickness of the tape in a 120-minute (C120) [986]compact
       cassette^[987][88]
     * 7 μm – diameter of the [988]nucleus of a typical eukaryotic
       [989]cell^[[990]citation needed]
     * about 7 μm – diameter of human [991]red blood cells^[992][89]
     * 3–8 μm – width of strand of [993]spider web silk^[994][90]
     * 5–10 μm – width of a [995]chloroplast^[996][91]
     * 8–11 μm – size of a ground-level fog or mist
       droplet^[997][92]^[998][note 4]
     * 7–12 μm – the diameter of human [999]white blood cells
     * 8–10 μm – the diameter of human [1000]macrophages

10 micrometers

   [[1001]edit]
   [1002][220px-FogParticlesHighSpeed.jpg] Fog particles are around
   10–50 μm (0.00039–0.00197 in) long.

   To help compare different [1003]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [1004]lengths between 10^−5 [1005]m and 10^−4 m (10 [1006]μm and
   100 μm).
     * 10 μm – width of [1007]cotton fibre^[1008][93]
     * 10 μm – [1009]tolerance of a [1010]Lego brick^[1011][94]
     * 10 μm – [1012]transistor width of the [1013]Intel 4004, the world's
       first commercial [1014]microprocessor
     * 10 μm – mean longest dimension of a human [1015]red blood
       cell^[[1016]citation needed]
     * 5–20 μm – [1017]dust mite excreta^[1018][95]
     * 10.6 μm – wavelength of light emitted by a [1019]carbon dioxide
       laser
     * 15 μm – width of [1020]silk fibre^[[1021]citation needed]
     * 17 μm – minimum width of a strand of human hair^[1022][25]
     * 17.6 μm – one [1023]twip, a unit of length in typography
     * 10 to 55 μm – width of [1024]wool fibre^[1025][93]
     * 25.4 μm – 1/1,000 inch, commonly referred to as 1 [1026]mil in the
       U.S. and 1 [1027]thou in the UK
     * 30 μm – length of a human [1028]skin cell
     * 30.8568 μm – 1 zeptoparsec
     * 50 μm – typical length of [1029]Euglena gracilis, a flagellate
       protist^[1030][96]
     * 50 μm – typical length of a human [1031]liver cell, an
       average-sized body cell^[[1032]citation needed]
     * 50 μm – length of a [1033]silt particle
     * 60 μm – length of a [1034]sperm cell
     * 78 μm — width of a pixel on the display of the iPhone 4, marketed
       as [1035]Retina Display^[1036][97]
     * 70 to 180 μm – thickness of paper

100 micrometers

   [[1037]edit]
   [1038][220px-Paramecium.jpg] A [1039]paramecium is around 300 μm
   (0.012 in) long.

   To help compare different [1040]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [1041]lengths between 10^−4 [1042]m and 10^−3 m (100 [1043]μm and
   1 [1044]mm). The term myriometer (abbr. mom, equivalent to 100
   micrometers; frequently confused with the [1045]myriameter, 10
   kilometers)^[1046][98] is deprecated; the decimal metric prefix
   [1047]myrio-^[1048][99] is obsolete^[1049][100]^[1050][101]^[1051][102]
   and was not included among the [1052]prefixes when the
   [1053]International System of Units was introduced in 1960.
     * 100 μm – 1/10 of a millimeter
     * 100 μm – 0.00394 inches
     * 100 μm – smallest distance that can be seen with the [1054]naked
       eye
     * 100 μm – average diameter of a strand of [1055]human
       hair^[1056][25]
     * 100 μm – thickness of a coat of [1057]paint
     * 100 μm – length of a [1058]dust particle
     * 120 μm – the [1059]geometric mean of the [1060]Planck length and
       the diameter of the [1061]observable universe: √8.8 × 10^26 m × 1.6
       × 10^−35 m
     * 120 μm – diameter of a [1062]human [1063]ovum
     * 170 μm – length of the largest mammalian [1064]sperm cell
       (rat)^[1065][103]
     * 170 μm – length of the largest [1066]sperm cell in nature,
       belonging to the [1067]Drosophila bifurca fruit
       fly^[1068][104]^[1069][105]
     * 181 μm – maximum width of a strand of human hair^[1070][25]
     * 100–400 μm – length of [1071]Demodex mites living in human
       [1072]hair follicles
     * 175–200 μm – typical thickness of a [1073]solar cell.
     * 200 μm – typical length of [1074]Paramecium caudatum, a ciliate
       protist
     * 200 μm – nominal width of the smallest commonly available
       mechanical pencil lead (0.2 mm)
     * 250–300 μm – length of a [1075]dust mite^[1076][106]
     * 340 μm – length of a [1077]pixel on a 17-inch monitor with a
       resolution of 1024×768
     * 500 μm – typical length of [1078]Amoeba proteus, an amoeboid
       protist
     * 500 μm – [1079]MEMS micro-engine^[1080][107]
     * 500 μm – average length of a grain of [1081]sand
     * 500 μm – average length of a grain of [1082]salt
     * 500 μm – average length of a grain of [1083]sugar
     * 560 μm – thickness of the central area of a human
       [1084]cornea^[1085][26]
     * 750 μm – diameter of a [1086]Thiomargarita namibiensis, the largest
       bacteria known^[1087][108]
     * 760 μm – thickness of an [1088]identification card

1 millimeter

   [[1089]edit]
   [1090][170px-Fire_ants_01.jpg] An average [1091]red ant is about 5 mm
   (0.20 in) long.
   "1mm" redirects here. For the Perfume song, see [1092]Level3 (Perfume
   album). For the measure, see [1093]millimetre.

   The [1094]millimetre
   ([1095]SI symbol: mm) is a [1096]unit of [1097]length in the
   [1098]metric system equal to 10^−3 [1099]metres (⁠1/1000⁠ m = 0.001 m).
   To help compare different [1100]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   lengths between 10^−3 m and 10^−2 m (1 mm and 1 cm).
     * 1.0 mm – 1/1,000 of a meter
     * 1.0 mm – 0.03937 [1101]inches or 5/127 (exactly)
     * 1.0 mm – side of a [1102]square of area [1103]1 mm²
     * 1.0 mm – diameter of a pinhead
     * 1.5 mm – average length of a flea^[1104][27]
     * 2.54 mm – distance between pins on old [1105]dual in-line package
       (DIP) electronic components
     * 5 mm – length of an average red ant
     * 5 mm – diameter of an average grain of rice
     * [1106]5.56×45mm NATO – standard ammunition size
     * 6 mm – approximate width of a pencil
     * 7 mm – length of a [1107]Paedophryne amauensis, the smallest-known
       vertebrate^[1108][109]
     * 7.1 mm – length of a sunflower seed
     * [1109]7.62×51mm NATO – common military ammunition size^[1110][110]
     * 8 mm – width of old-format home movie film
     * 8 mm – length of a [1111]Paedocypris progenetica, the
       smallest-known fish^[1112][111]

1 centimeter

   [[1113]edit]
   [1114][220px-Fingernail_label_%28enwiki%29.jpg] An average human
   [1115]fingernail is 1 cm (0.39 in) wide
   "10 mm" redirects here. For firearms cartridges, see [1116]10 mm
   calibre.

   The [1117]centimetre
   ([1118]SI symbol: cm) is a [1119]unit of [1120]length in the
   [1121]metric system equal to 10^−2 [1122]metres (⁠1/100⁠ m = 0.01 m).
   To help compare different [1123]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   lengths between 10^−2 m and 10^−1 m (1 cm and 1 dm).
     * 1 cm – 10 [1124]millimeters
     * 1 cm – 0.39 [1125]inches
     * 1 cm – edge of a [1126]square of area [1127]1 cm^2
     * 1 cm – edge of a [1128]cube of volume [1129]1 mL
     * 1 cm – length of a coffee bean
     * 1 cm – approximate width of average fingernail
     * 1.2 cm – length of a bee
     * 1.2 cm – diameter of a die
     * 1.5 cm – length of a very large mosquito
     * 1.6 cm – length of a Jaragua Sphaero, a very small reptile
     * 1.7 cm – length of a Thorius arboreus, the smallest
       salamander^[1130][112]
     * 2 cm – approximate width of an adult human finger
     * 2.54 cm – 1 inch
     * 3.08568 cm – 1 [1131]attoparsec
     * 3.4 cm – length of a quail egg^[1132][113]
     * 3.5 cm – width of film commonly used in motion pictures and still
       photography
     * 3.78 cm – amount of distance the Moon moves away from Earth each
       year^[1133][114]
     * 4.3 cm – minimum diameter of a golf ball^[1134][115]
     * 5 cm – usual diameter of a chicken egg
     * 5 cm – height of a hummingbird, the smallest-known bird
     * 5.08 cm – 2 [1135]inches,
     * 5.5 × 5.5 × 5.5 cm – dimensions of a 3x3x3 Rubik's cube
     * 6.1 cm – average height of an apple
     * 7.3–7.5 cm – diameter of a baseball^[1136][28]
     * 8.6 cm × 5.4 cm – dimensions of a standard credit card (also called
       CR80)^[1137][116]^[1138][117]
     * 9 cm – length of a speckled padloper, the smallest-known turtle

1 decimeter

   [[1139]edit]
   "10cm" redirects here. For the band, see [1140]10cm (band).
   [1141][220px-Foot_on_white_background.jpg] An adult human foot is about
   28 cm (11 in) long.

   The [1142]decimetre
   ([1143]SI symbol: dm) is a [1144]unit of [1145]length in the
   [1146]metric system equal to 10^−1 [1147]metres (⁠1/10⁠ m = 0.1 m). To
   help compare different [1148]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   lengths between 10 [1149]centimeters and 100 centimeters (10^−1 meter
   and 1 meter).

Conversions

   [[1150]edit]

   10 centimeters (abbreviated to 10 cm) is equal to:
     * 1 [1151]decimeter (dm), a term not in common use (1 [1152]L = 1
       dm^3.)
     * 100 [1153]millimeters
     * 3.9 [1154]inches
     * a side of a [1155]square of area [1156]0.01 m^2
     * the edge of a [1157]cube with a volume of [1158]1×10^−3 m^3 (1 L)

Wavelengths

   [[1159]edit]
     * 10 cm = 1.0 dm – wavelength of the highest [1160]UHF radio
       frequency, [1161]3 GHz
     * 12 cm = 1.2 dm – wavelength of the [1162]2.45 GHz ISM radio band
     * 21 cm = 2.1 dm – wavelength of the [1163]1.4 GHz hydrogen emission
       line, a [1164]hyperfine transition of the hydrogen atom
     * 100 cm = 10 dm – wavelength of the lowest UHF radio frequency,
       [1165]300 MHz

Human-defined scales and structures

   [[1166]edit]
     * 10.16 cm = 1.016 dm – 1 [1167]hand used in measuring height of
       horses (4 inches)
     * 12 cm = 1.2 dm – diameter of a compact disc (CD) (= 120 mm)
     * 15 cm = 1.5 dm – length of a [1168]Bic pen with cap on
     * 22 cm = 2.2 dm – diameter of a typical association football (soccer
       ball)
     * 30 cm = 3 dm – typical school-use ruler length (= 300 mm)
     * 30.48 cm = 3.048 dm – 1 [1169]foot (measure)
     * 60 cm = 6 dm – standard depth (front to back) of a domestic kitchen
       worktop in Europe (= 600 mm)
     * 90 cm = 9 dm – average length of a rapier, a fencing
       sword^[1170][30]
     * 91.44 cm = 9.144 dm – one [1171]yard (measure)

Nature

   [[1172]edit]
     * 10 cm = 1 dm – diameter of the human [1173]cervix upon entering the
       second stage of labour
     * 11 cm = 1.1 dm – length of an average potato in the US
     * 13 cm = 1.3 dm – body length of a [1174]Goliath birdeater
     * 15 cm = 1.5 dm – approximate size of largest beetle species
     * 19 cm = 1.9 dm – length of a banana
     * 26.3 cm = 2.6 dm – length of average male human foot
     * 29.98 cm = 2.998 dm – distance light in vacuum travels in one
       [1175]nanosecond
     * 30 cm = 3.0 dm – maximum leg length of a Goliath birdeater
     * 31 cm = 3.1 dm – wingspan of largest butterfly species
       [1176]Ornithoptera alexandrae
     * 32 cm – length of the [1177]Goliath frog, the world's largest frog
     * 46 cm = 4.6 dm – length of an average domestic cat
     * 50 to 65 cm = 5–6.5 dm – a coati's tail
     * 66 cm = 6.6 dm – length of the longest pine cones (produced by the
       sugar pine^[1178][118])

1 meter

   [[1180]edit]
   "1m" redirects here. For other uses, see [1181]1M.
   [1182][220px-Da_Vinci_Vitruve_Luc_Viatour.jpg] Leonardo da Vinci drew
   the [1183]Vitruvian Man within a square of side 1.83 m (6 ft 0 in) and
   a circle about 1.2 m (3 ft 11 in) in radius.

   To help compare different [1184]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   lengths between one [1185]meter and ten meters. Light, in vacuum,
   travels 1 meter in
   1⁄299,792,458, or 3.3356409519815E-9 of a second.

Conversions

   [[1186]edit]

   1 [1187]meter is:
     * 10 [1188]decimeters
     * 100 [1189]centimeters
     * 1,000 [1190]millimeters
     * 39.37 [1191]inches
     * 3.28 [1192]feet
     * 1.1 [1193]yards
     * side of [1194]square with area [1195]1 m^2
     * edge of [1196]cube with surface area [1197]6 m^2 and volume [1198]1
       m^3
     * radius of [1199]circle with area [1200]π m^2
     * radius of [1201]sphere with surface area [1202]4π m^2 and volume
       [1203]4/3π m^3

1 decameter

   [[1245]edit]

   [1246][220px-Image-Blue_Whale_and_Hector_Dolphine_Colored.jpg] A
   [1247]blue whale has been measured as 33 m (108 ft) long; this drawing
   compares its length to that of a human diver and a dolphin.

   The [1248]decametre
   ([1249]SI symbol: dam) is a [1250]unit of [1251]length in the
   [1252]metric system equal to 10 [1253]meters (10^1 m). To help compare
   different [1254]orders of magnitude, this section lists lengths between
   10 and 100 meters.

Conversions

   [[1255]edit]

   10 meters (very rarely termed a [1256]decameter which is abbreviated as
   dam) is equal to:
     * 10 meters
     * 100 [1257]decimeters
     * 1,000 [1258]centimeters
     * 10,000 [1259]millimeters
     * 10,000,000 [1260]micrometers (or rarely 10,000,000 microns)
     * 32.8 [1261]feet
     * 11 [1262]yards
     * side of a [1263]square with area [1264]100 m²

1 hectometer

   [[1326]edit]

   [1327][220px-Cheops_pyramid_01.jpg] The Great Pyramid of Giza is
   138.8 m (455 ft) high.[1328][170px-M27_DLS.JPG] British [1329]driver
   location sign and location marker post on the [1330]M27 in
   [1331]Hampshire. The location marker posts are installed at 100-meter
   intervals.^[1332][131]

   The [1333]hectometre
   ([1334]SI symbol: hm) is a [1335]unit of [1336]length in the
   [1337]metric system equal to 100 [1338]meters (10^2 m). To compare
   different [1339]orders of magnitude this section lists lengths between
   100 meters and 1,000 meters (1 [1340]kilometer).

Conversions

   [[1341]edit]

   100 meters (sometimes termed a hectometer) is equal to:
     * 328 [1342]feet
     * one side of a [1343]1 hectare square
     * a fifth of a modern [1344]li, a Chinese unit of measurement
     * the approximate distance travelled by light in 300
       [1345]nanoseconds

1 kilometer

   [[1401]edit]

   [1402][220px-Fuji_Kawaguchi_357.JPG] [1403]Mount Fuji is 3.776
   kilometres (2.346 mi) high.

   The [1404]kilometre
   ([1405]SI symbol: km) is a [1406]unit of [1407]length in the
   [1408]metric system equal to 1000 [1409]meters (10^3 m). To help
   compare different [1410]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [1411]lengths between 1 kilometer and 10 [1412]kilometers (10^3 and
   10^4 [1413]meters).

Conversions

   [[1414]edit]

   1 [1415]kilometer (unit symbol km) is equal to:
     * 1,000 [1416]meters
     * 0.621371 [1417]miles
     * 1,093.61 [1418]yards
     * 3,280.84 [1419]feet
     * 39,370.1 [1420]inches
     * 100,000 [1421]centimeters
     * 1,000,000 [1422]millimeters
     * Side of a [1423]square of area [1424]1 [1425]km^2
     * Radius of a [1426]circle of area [1427]π km^2

Human-defined scales and structures

   [[1428]edit]

     * 1 km – wavelength of the highest [1429]long wave radio frequency,
       [1430]300 [1431]kHz^[1432][138]
     * 1.008 km – proposed height of the [1433]Jeddah Tower, a
       [1434]megatall skyscraper under construction in [1435]Saudi Arabia
     * 1.280 km – span of the [1436]Golden Gate Bridge (distance between
       towers)^[1437][139]
     * 1.609 km – 1 statute mile
     * 1.852 km – 1 [1438]nautical mile, equal to 1 [1439]arcminute of
       [1440]latitude at the surface of the Earth^[1441][140]
     * 1.991 km – span of the [1442]Akashi Kaikyō Bridge^[1443][141]
     * 2.309 km – axial length of the [1444]Three Gorges Dam, the largest
       [1445]dam in the world located in [1446]China^[1447][34]
     * 3.991 km – length of the [1448]Akashi Kaikyō Bridge, longest
       [1449]suspension bridge in the world as of
       December 2008^[1450][update]^[1451][142]
     * 4 km – width of [1452]Central Park
     * 5.072 km – elevation of [1453]Tanggula Mountain Pass, below highest
       peak in the [1454]Tanggula Mountains, highest [1455]railway pass in
       the world as of August 2005^[1456][update]^[1457][143]
     * 5.8 km – elevation of Cerro Aucanquilcha, highest road in the
       world, located in [1458]Chile^[1459][144]
     * [1460]98 airports have paved runways from 4 km to 5.5 km in length.
     * 8 km – length of [1461]Palm Jebel Ali, an [1462]artificial island
       built off the coast of [1463]Dubai
     * 9.8 km – length of [1464]The World, an artificial archipelago that
       is also built off the coast of [1465]Dubai, whose islands resemble
       a [1466]world map

10 kilometers (1 myriameter)

   [[1528]edit]

   [1529][220px-Strait_of_Gibraltar_5.53940W_35.97279N.jpg] The
   [1530]Strait of Gibraltar is 13 km (8.1 mi) wide.

   To help compare different [1531]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [1532]lengths between 10 and 100 [1533]kilometers (10^4 to 10^5
   [1534]meters). The myriameter^[1535][148] (sometimes also spelled
   myriometer; 10,000 meters) is a deprecated unit name; the decimal
   metric prefix [1536]myria-^[1537][99] (sometimes also written as
   [1538]myrio-^[1539][149]^[1540][150]^[1541][151]) is
   obsolete^[1542][100]^[1543][101]^[1544][102] and was not included among
   the [1545]prefixes when the [1546]International System of Units was
   introduced in 1960.

Conversions

   [[1547]edit]

   10 kilometers is equal to:
   [1548][220px-Myriameterstein36R%C3%BCdesheimRhein.JPG] [1549]Distance
   marker on the [1550]Rhine: 36 (XXXVI) myriameters from [1551]Basel. The
   stated distance is 360 km (220 mi); the comma is the [1552]decimal
   separator in [1553]Germany.
     * 10,000 [1554]meters
     * About 6.2 [1555]miles
     * 1 mil (the [1556]Scandinavian mile), now standardized as 10 km:
          + 1 mil, the unit of measure commonly used in [1557]Norway and
            [1558]Sweden^[1559][152] used to be 11,295 m in Norway and
            10,688 m in Sweden.
     * [1560]farsang, unit of measure commonly used in [1561]Iran and
       [1562]Turkey^[1563][153]

100 kilometers

   [[1618]edit]

   [1619][170px-Suez_canal_30.55N_32.28E.jpg] The [1620]Suez Canal is
   163 km (101 mi) long.

   A length of 100 kilometers (about 62 miles), as a rough amount, is
   relatively common in measurements on Earth and for some astronomical
   objects. It is the altitude at which the [1621]FAI defines
   [1622]spaceflight to begin.

   To help compare [1623]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [1624]lengths between 100 and 1,000 [1625]kilometers (10^5 and 10^6
   [1626]meters).

Conversions

   [[1627]edit]

   A distance of 100 kilometers is equal to about 62 miles (or 62.13711922
   miles).

1 megameter

   [[1711]edit]

   [1712][220px-1e6m_comparison_Mars_Mercury_Moon_Pluto_Haumea_-_no_transp
   arency.png] Small planets, the [1713]Moon and [1714]dwarf planets in
   the Solar System have diameters from one to ten million meters. Top
   row: [1715]Mars (left), [1716]Mercury (right); bottom row: [1717]Moon
   (left), [1718]Pluto (center), and [1719]Haumea (right), to scale.

   The megametre
   ([1720]SI symbol: Mm) is a [1721]unit of [1722]length in the
   [1723]metric system equal to 1000000 [1724]meters (10^6 m). To help
   compare different [1725]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [1726]lengths starting at 10^6 [1727]m ([1728]1 Mm or 1,000 [1729]km).

Conversions

   [[1730]edit]

   1 megameter is equal to:
     * 1000 km
     * 1 E+6 m (one million meters)
     * approximately 621.37 [1731]miles
     * 1 E+12 μm (one trillion micrometers)
     * Side of [1732]square with area 1,000,000 km^2

Human-defined scales and structures

   [[1733]edit]

     * 2.100 Mm – length of proposed [1734]Iran-Pakistan-India gas pipe
     * 2.100 Mm – distance from [1735]Casablanca to [1736]Rome
     * 2.288 Mm – length of the official [1737]Alaska Highway when it was
       built in the 1940s^[1738][162]
     * 3.069 Mm – length of [1739]Interstate 95 (from [1740]Houlton,
       Maine, to [1741]Miami, Florida)
     * 3.846 Mm – length of [1742]U.S. Route 1 (from [1743]Fort Kent,
       Maine, to [1744]Key West, Florida)
     * 5.000 Mm – width of the [1745]United States
     * 5.007 Mm – estimated length of [1746]Interstate 90 (Seattle,
       Washington, to Boston, Massachusetts)
     * 5.614 Mm – length of the Australian [1747]Dingo Fence^[1748][163]
     * 6.371 Mm – global-average [1749]Earth radius
     * 6.4 Mm – length of the [1750]Great Wall of China
     * 7.821 Mm – length of the [1751]Trans-Canada Highway, the world's
       longest national highway (from [1752]Victoria, British Columbia, to
       [1753]St. John's, Newfoundland)
     * 8.836 Mm – road distance between [1754]Prudhoe Bay, Alaska, and
       [1755]Key West, Florida, the endpoints of the U.S. road network
     * 8.852 Mm – aggregate length of the [1756]Great Wall of China,
       including trenches, hills and rivers^[1757][164]
     * 9.259 Mm – length of the [1758]Trans-Siberian railway^[1759][165]

10 megameters

   [[1837]edit]

   [1838][220px-1e7m_comparison_Uranus_Neptune_Sirius_B_Earth_Venus.png]
   Planets from Venus up to Uranus have diameters from ten to one hundred
   million meters. Top row: [1839]Uranus (left), [1840]Neptune (right);
   middle row: [1841]Earth (left), [1842]Sirius B (center), and
   [1843]Venus (right), to scale.

   To help compare different [1848]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [1849]lengths starting at 10^7 [1850]meters ([1851]10 megameters or
   10,000 [1852]kilometers).

Conversions

   [[1853]edit]

   10 megameters (10 Mm) is
     * 6,215 [1854]miles
     * side of a [1855]square of area 100,000,000 [1856]square kilometers
       (km^2)
     * radius of a [1857]circle of area 314,159,265 km^2

       Verne, [1871]Twenty Thousand Leagues Under the Seas)

100 megameters

   [[1896]edit]

   [1897][220px-1e8m_comparison_Saturn_Jupiter_OGLE-TR-122b_with_Uranus_Ne
   ptune_Sirius_B_Earth_Venus_no_transparency.png] The
   [1898]Earth-[1899]Moon orbit, [1900]Saturn, [1901]OGLE-TR-122b,
   [1902]Jupiter, and [1903]other objects, to scale. Click on image for
   detailed view and links to other length
   scales.[1904][300px-Scale_model_of_Solar_System_10_billion_to_1.svg.png
   ] Scale model at megameters of the main Solar System bodies

   To help compare different [1905]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [1906]lengths starting at 10^8 [1907]meters ([1908]100 megameters or
   100,000 [1909]kilometers or 62,150 [1910]miles).
     * 102 Mm – diameter of [1911]HD 149026 b, an unusually dense
       [1912]Jovian planet
     * 115 Mm – width of Saturn's Rings
     * 120 Mm – diameter of [1913]EBLM J0555-57Ab, the smallest-known star
     * 120 Mm – diameter of [1914]Saturn
     * 142 Mm – diameter of [1915]Jupiter, the largest planet in the
       [1916]Solar System
     * 170 Mm – diameter of [1917]TRAPPIST-1, a star discovered to have
       seven planets around it
     * 174 Mm – diameter of [1918]OGLE-TR-122b, one of the smallest known
       stars
     * 180 Mm – average distance covered during life
     * 215 Mm – diameter of [1919]Proxima Centauri, the nearest star to
       the Solar System
     * 257 Mm – diameter of [1920]TrES-4, one of the largest exoplanets
     * 260 Mm – diameter of the [1921]Barnard's Star
     * 272 Mm – diameter of [1922]WASP-12b
     * 299.792 Mm – one [1923]light-second; the distance [1924]light
       travels in [1925]vacuum in one [1926]second (see [1927]speed of
       light)
     * 314 Mm – diameter of [1928]CT Cha b
     * 384.4 Mm (238,855 mi) – average [1929]Earth–Moon
       distance^[1930][168]
     * 671 Mm – separation between [1931]Jupiter and [1932]Europa
     * 696 Mm – [1933]radius of [1934]Sun
     * 989 Mm – diameter of [1935]Epsilon Indi, one of the nearest stars
       to Earth

1 gigameter

   [[1936]edit]

   [1937][220px-Gigameter_group.png] 13 things in the gigameter
   group[1938][220px-1e9m_comparison_Gamma_Orionis%2C_Algol_B%2C_the_Sun%2
   C_and_smaller_-_antialiased_no_transparency.png] Upper part:
   [1939]Gamma Orionis, [1940]Algol B, the [1941]Sun (centre), and
   [1942]other objects to scale.

   The gigametre
   ([1943]SI symbol: Gm) is a [1944]unit of [1945]length in the
   [1946]metric system equal to 1000000000 [1947]meters (10^9 m). To help
   compare different distances this section lists lengths starting at 10^9
   [1948]meters (1 gigameter (Gm) or 1 billion [1949]meters).
     * 1.2 Gm – separation between [1950]Saturn and [1951]Titan
     * 1.39 Gm – diameter of [1952]Sun^[1953][169]^[1954][170]
     * 1.5 Gm – orbit from Earth of the [1955]James Webb Space Telescope
     * 1.71 Gm – diameter of [1956]Alpha Centauri A, one of the closest
       stars.^[1957][171]
     * 2.19 Gm – closest approach of [1958]Comet Lexell to [1959]Earth,
       happened on 1 July 1770; closest [1960]comet approach on record
     * 2.38 Gm – diameter of [1961]Sirius A, brightest naked eye
       star.^[1962][172]
     * 3 Gm – total length of "wiring" in the human brain^[1963][173]
     * 3.5 Gm – diameter of [1964]Vega^[1965][174]
     * 4.2 Gm – diameter of [1966]Algol B
     * 4.3 Gm – circumference of [1967]Sun
     * 5.0 Gm – closest approach of [1968]Comet Halley to Earth, happened
       on 10 April 837
     * 5.0 Gm – (proposed) Size of the arms of the giant triangle shaped
       Michelson interferometer of the [1969]Laser Interferometer Space
       Antenna (LISA) planned to start observations sometime in the 2030s.
     * 7.9 Gm – diameter of [1970]Gamma Orionis, a blue dwarf or blue
       giant
     * 9.0 Gm – estimated diameter of the [1971]event horizon of
       [1972]Sagittarius A*, the [1973]supermassive black hole in the
       center of the [1974]Milky Way galaxyrv

10 gigameters

   [[1975]edit]

   [1976][220px-1e10m_comparison_Rigel%2C_Aldebaran%2C_and_smaller_-_antia
   liased_no_transparency.png] Rigel and Aldebaran (top left and right)
   compared to smaller stars, the Sun (very small dot in lower middle,
   with orbit of Mercury as yellow ellipse) and transparent sphere with
   radius of one light-minute

   To help compare different [1977]distances this section lists lengths
   starting at 10^10 [1978]meters (10 [1979]gigameters (Gm) or 10 million
   [1980]kilometers, or 0.07 [1981]astronomical units).
     * 10.4 Gm – diameter of Spica, an oval-shaped blue giant star and a
       [1982]nearby supernova candidate.^[1983][175]
     * 12.6 Gm – diameter of [1984]Pollux, the closest [1985]red giant
       [1986]star to the Sun.^[1987][176] It is a [1988]red clump star
       fusing helium into carbon at its core.^[1989][177]
     * 15 Gm – closest distance of [1990]Comet Hyakutake from [1991]Earth
     * 18 Gm – one [1992]light-minute (see yellow sphere in right-hand
       diagram)
     * 24 Gm – radius of a [1993]heliostationary orbit
     * 30.8568 Gm – 1 microparsec
     * 35 Gm – approximate diameter of [1994]Arcturus, a close red giant
       star.^[1995][178] It is on the [1996]red giant branch, fusing
       hydrogen into helium in a shell surrounding an inert helium
       core.^[1997][177]
     * 46 Gm – [1998]perihelion distance of [1999]Mercury (yellow ellipse
       on the right)
     * 55 Gm – 60,000-year [2000]perigee of [2001]Mars (last achieved on
       27 August 2003)
     * 58 Gm – average passing distance between [2002]Earth and Mars at
       the moment they overtake each other in their orbits
     * 61 Gm – diameter of [2003]Aldebaran, a red giant branch star (large
       star on right)^[2004][179]
     * 70 Gm – [2005]aphelion distance of Mercury
     * 76 Gm – [2006]Neso's [2007]apocentric distance; greatest distance
       of a [2008]natural satellite from its parent [2009]planet
       ([2010]Neptune)

100 gigameters

   [[2011]edit]

   [2012][220px-1e11m_comparison_R_Doradus_and_Betelgeuse%2C_and_smaller_-
   _antialiased_no_transparency.png] From largest to smallest: Jupiter's
   orbit, red supergiant star Betelgeuse, Mars' orbit, Earth's orbit, star
   R Doradus, and orbits of Venus, Mercury. Inside R Doradus's depiction
   are the blue supergiant star Rigel and red giant star Aldebaran. The
   faint yellow glow around the Sun represents one light-minute. Click
   image to see more details and links to their scales.

   To help compare distances at different [2013]orders of magnitude this
   section lists lengths starting at 10^11 [2014]meters (100
   [2015]gigameter or 100 million [2016]kilometers or 0.7
   [2017]astronomical units).
     * 103 Gm (0.69 au) – diameter of [2018]Rigel^[2019][176]
     * 109 Gm (0.7 au) – distance between Venus and the Sun
     * 149.6 Gm (93.0 million mi; 1.0 au) – average distance between the
       [2020]Earth and the Sun – the original definition of the
       [2021]astronomical unit
     * 199 Gm (1.3 au) – diameter of [2022]Rho Persei, an [2023]asymptotic
       giant branch star, fusing carbon into neon in a shell surrounding
       an inert core.^[2024][180]
     * 228 Gm (1.5 au) – distance between [2025]Mars and the Sun
     * 248 Gm (1.7 au) – diameter of [2026]Enif, a small [2027]red
       supergiant star in the constellation [2028]Pegasus^[2029][181]
     * 280 Gm (1.9 au) – diameter of [2030]Deneb, a blue supergiant and
       the brightest star in the [2031]Cygnus constellation^[2032][182]
     * 511 Gm (3.4 au) – average diameter of [2033]Mira, a pulsating red
       giant and the progenitor of the [2034]Mira variables. It is an
       [2035]asymptotic giant branch star.^[2036][183]
     * 570 Gm (3.8 au) – length of the tail of [2037]Comet Hyakutake
       measured by [2038]Ulysses; the actual value could be much higher
     * 590 Gm (3.9 au) – diameter of the [2039]Pistol Star, a blue
       [2040]hypergiant star^[2041][184]
     * 591 Gm (4.0 au) – minimum distance between the [2042]Earth and
       [2043]Jupiter
     * 780 Gm (5.2 au) – average distance between Jupiter and the Sun
     * 785 Gm (5.25 au) – diameter of [2044]Rho Cassiopeiae, a rare
       [2045]yellow hypergiant star^[2046][185]
     * 947 Gm (6.4 au) – diameter of [2047]Antares A
     * 965 Gm (6.4 au) – maximum distance between the Earth and Jupiter

1 terameter

   [[2048]edit]

   [2049][220px-Terameter_group.png] Eight things in the terameter
   group[2050][220px-1e12m_comparison_Kuiper_belt_and_smaller.png]
   Comparison of size of the Kuiper belt (large faint torus) with the star
   VY Canis Majoris (within Saturn's orbit), Betelgeuse (inside Jupiter's
   orbit) and R Doradus (small central red sphere) together with the
   orbits of Neptune and Uranus, to scale. The yellow ellipses represent
   the orbits of each planet and the dwarf planet Pluto.

   The terametre
   ([2051]SI symbol: Tm) is a [2052]unit of [2053]length in the
   [2054]metric system equal to 1000000000000 [2055]meters (10^12 m). To
   help compare different [2056]distances, this section lists lengths
   starting at 10^12 [2057]m (1 [2058]Tm or 1 billion [2059]km or 6.7
   [2060]astronomical units).
     * ≈1 Tm – 6.7 au – diameter of the red supergiant [2061]Betelgeuse
       based on multiple angular diameter estimates^[2062][186]
     * 1.032 Tm – 6.9 au – diameter of the blue hypergiant [2063]Eta
       Carinae (at optical depth 2/3)^[2064][187]
     * 1.079 Tm – 7.2 au – one [2065]light-hour
     * 1.114 Tm – 7.5 au – diameter of [2066]WOH G64, a star in the
       [2067]Large Magellanic Cloud, which recently transformed from a red
       hypergiant to a yellow hypergiant^[2068][188]
     * 1.4 Tm – 9.5 au – average distance between [2069]Saturn and the
       [2070]Sun
     * 1.47 Tm – 9.9 au – diameter of [2071]HR 5171 A, a [2072]yellow
       hypergiant star.^[2073][189]
     * 1.5 Tm – 10 au – estimated diameter of [2074]VV Cephei A, a red
       hypergiant with a blue dwarf companion.^[2075][190]
     * 1.75 Tm – 11.7 au – estimated diameter of [2076]Mu Cephei, a red
       supergiant (possibly hypergiant) among the [2077]largest-known
       stars.^[2078][191]
     * 2 Tm – 13.2 au – estimated diameter of [2079]VY Canis Majoris, a
       red hypergiant that is among the [2080]largest-known
       stars^[2081][192]^[2082][193]
     * 2.142 Tm – 14.3 au – estimated diameter of [2083]WOH G64, prior to
       its transformation into a yellow hypergiant.
     * 2.9 Tm – 19.4 au – average distance between [2084]Uranus and the
       Sun
     * 4.4 Tm – 29.4 au – [2085]perihelion distance of [2086]Pluto
     * 4.5 Tm – 30.1 au – average distance between [2087]Neptune and the
       Sun
     * 4.5 Tm – 30.1 au – inner radius of the [2088]Kuiper belt
     * 5.7 Tm – 38.1 au – perihelion distance of [2089]Eris
     * 6.0 Tm – 40.5 au – distance from [2090]Earth at which the
       [2091]Pale Blue Dot photograph was taken.
     * 7.3 Tm – 48.8 au – [2092]aphelion distance of [2093]Pluto
     * 7.5 Tm – 50.1 au – outer boundary of the [2094]Kuiper Belt

     * 16.5 Tm – 111 AU – distance to [2118]Pioneer 10 as of March 2014
     * 16.6 Tm – 111.2 AU – distance to [2119]Voyager 2 as of May 2016

1 petameter

   [[2144]edit]

   [2145][220px-1e15m_comparison_cat%27s_eye_nebula_barnard_68_one_light_y
   ear.png] Largest circle with yellow arrow indicates one
   [2146]light-year from [2147]Sun; [2148]Cat's Eye Nebula on left and
   [2149]Barnard 68 in middle are depicted in front of [2150]Comet 1910
   A1's orbit. Click image for larger view, details and links to other
   scales.

   The petametre
   ([2151]SI symbol: Pm) is a [2152]unit of [2153]length in the
   [2154]metric system equal to 10^15 [2155]meters. To help compare
   different [2156]distances this section lists lengths starting at 10^15
   [2157]m (1 Pm or 1 trillion [2158]km or 6685 [2159]astronomical units
   (AU) or 0.11 [2160]light-years).
     * 1.0 Pm = 0.105702341 light-years
     * 1.9 Pm ± 0.5 Pm = 12,000 AU = 0.2 light-year radius of [2161]Cat's
       Eye Nebula's inner core^[2162][195]^[2163][196]
     * 3.08568 Pm = 20,626 AU = 1 deciparsec
     * 4.7 Pm = 30,000 AU = half-light-year diameter of [2164]Bok globule
       [2165]Barnard 68^[2166][197]
     * 7.5 Pm – 50,000 AU – possible outer boundary of [2167]Oort cloud
       (other estimates are 75,000 to 125,000 or even 189,000 [2168]AU
       (1.18, 2, and 3 [2169]light-years, respectively))
     * 9.5 Pm – 63,241.1 AU – one [2170]light-year, the distance light
       travels in one year
     * 9.9 Pm – 66,000 AU – [2171]aphelion distance of the [2172]C/1999 F1
       (Catalina)

1 exameter

   [[2208]edit]

   [2209][220px-1e18m_comparison_1000_light_years_nebula_clusters.png]
   Lengths with order of magnitude 1e18m: thousand light-year radius
   circle with yellow arrow and 100 light-year circle at right with
   globular cluster Messier 5 within and Carina Nebula in front; globular
   cluster Omega Centauri to left of both; part of the
   1,400-light-year-wide Tarantula Nebula fills the background

   The exametre
   ([2210]SI symbol: Em) is a [2211]unit of [2212]length in the
   [2213]metric system equal to 10^18 [2214]meters. To help compare
   different [2215]distances this section lists lengths between
   10^18 [2216]m (1 [2217]Em or 105.7 [2218]light-years) and 10^19 m
   (10 Em or 1,057 light-years).
     * 1.2 Em – 129 light-years – diameter of [2219]Messier 13 (a typical
       [2220]globular cluster)
     * 1.6 Em – 172 ± 12.5 light-years – diameter of [2221]Omega Centauri
       (one of the largest-known [2222]globular clusters, perhaps
       containing over a million [2223]stars)^[2224][200]^[2225][201]
     * 3.08568 Em – 326.1 light-years – 1 hectoparsec
     * 3.1 Em – 310 light-years – distance to [2226]Canopus according to
       [2227]Hipparcos^[2228][202]
     * 3.9 Em – 410 light-years – distance to [2229]Betelgeuse according
       to Hipparcos^[2230][203]
     * 6.2 Em – 650 light-years – distance to the [2231]Helix Nebula,
       located in the constellation [2232]Aquarius^[2233][204]
     * 8.2 Em – 860 light-years – distance to [2234]Rigel according to
       Hipparcos^[2235][202]
     * 9.4 Em — 1 light-millennium – 1000 light-years

1 zettameter

   [[2278]edit]

   The zettametre
   ([2279]SI symbol: Zm) is a [2280]unit of [2281]length in the
   [2282]metric system equal to 10^21 [2283]meters.^[2284][56] To help
   compare different [2285]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [2286]distances starting at 1 [2287]Zm (10^21 [2288]m or 110,000
   [2289]light-years).
     * 1.7 Zm – 179,000 light-years – distance to the [2290]Large
       Magellanic Cloud, largest [2291]satellite galaxy of the [2292]Milky
       Way
     * <1.9 Zm – <200,000 light-years – revised estimated diameter of the
       disc of the [2293]Milky Way Galaxy. The size was previously thought
       to be half of this.
     * 2.0 Zm – 210,000 light-years – distance to the [2294]Small
       Magellanic Cloud
     * 2.8 Zm – 300,000 light-years – distance to the [2295]Intergalactic
       Wanderer, one of the most distant [2296]globular clusters of Milky
       Way
     * 8.5 Zm – 900,000 light-years – distance to the [2297]Leo I Dwarf
       Galaxy, farthest-known [2298]Milky Way satellite [2299]galaxy

10 zettameters

   [[2300]edit]

   To help compare different [2301]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [2302]distances starting at 10 [2303]Zm (10^22 [2304]m or 1.1 million
   [2305]light-years).
     * 24 Zm – 2.5 million light-years – distance to the [2306]Andromeda
       Galaxy, the nearest major [2307]galaxy.
     * 30.8568 Zm – 3.2616 million light-years – 1 [2308]megaparsec
     * 40 Zm – 4.2 million light-years – distance to the [2309]IC 10, a
       distant member of the [2310]Local Group of [2311]galaxies
     * 49.2 Zm – 5.2 million light-years – width of the [2312]Local Group
       of [2313]galaxies
     * 95 Zm – 10 million light-years – distance to the [2314]Sculptor
       Galaxy in the [2315]Sculptor Group of galaxies
     * 95 Zm – 10 million light-years – distance to the [2316]Maffei 1,
       the nearest giant [2317]elliptical galaxy in the [2318]Maffei 1
       Group

     * 800 Zm – 85 million light-years – approximate distance to the
       [2331]Eridanus cluster

1 yottameter

   [[2332]edit]

   The yottametre
   ([2333]SI symbol: Ym) is a [2334]unit of [2335]length in the
   [2336]metric system equal to 10^24 [2337]meters.^[2338][56]

   To help compare different [2339]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [2340]distances starting at 1 Ym (10^24 [2341]m or 105.702 million
   [2342]light-years).
     * 1.2 Ym – 127 million light-years – distance to the closest observed
       [2343]gamma ray burst [2344]GRB 980425
     * 1.3 Ym – 137 million light-years – distance to the [2345]Centaurus
       Cluster of [2346]galaxies, the nearest large [2347]supercluster
     * 1.9 Ym – 201 million light-years – diameter of the [2348]Local
       Supercluster
     * 2.17 Ym – 1 light-galactic-years – 230 million light-years
     * 2.3 Ym – 225 to 250 million light-years – distance light travels in
       vacuum in one [2349]galactic year
     * 2.8 Ym – 296 million light-years – distance to the [2350]Coma
       Cluster
     * 3.15 Ym – 330 million light years – diameter of the [2351]Boötes
       Void
     * 3.2 Ym – 338 million light-years – distance to [2352]Stephan's
       Quintet
     * 4.7 Ym – 496 million light-years – length of the [2353]CfA2 Great
       Wall, one of the largest observed superstructures in the
       [2354]Universe
     * 6.1 Ym – 645 million light-years – distance to the [2355]Shapley
       Supercluster
     * 9.5 Ym – 996 million light-years – diameter of the [2356]Eridanus
       Supervoid

10 yottameters

   [[2357]edit]

   [2358][220px-Superclusters_atlasoftheuniverse.gif] The universe within
   one billion light-years of Earth

   To help compare different [2359]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [2360]distances starting at 10 [2361]Ym (10^25 [2362]m or 1.1 billion
   [2363]light-years). At this scale, expansion of the [2364]universe
   becomes significant. Distance of these objects are derived from their
   measured [2365]redshifts, which depends on the [2366]cosmological
   models used.
     * 13 Ym – 1.37 billion light-years – length of the [2367]South Pole
       Wall
     * 13 Ym – 1.38 billion light-years – length of the [2368]Sloan Great
       Wall
     * 18 Ym – redshift 0.16 – 1.9 billion light-years – distance to the
       [2369]quasar [2370]3C 273 ([2371]light travel distance)
     * 30.8568 Ym – 3.2616 billion light-years – 1 gigaparsec
     * 31.2204106 Ym − 3.3 billion light-years − length of [2372]The Giant
       Arc, a large cosmic structure discovered in 2021
     * 33 Ym – 3.5 billion light-years – maximum distance of the [2373]2dF
       Galaxy Redshift Survey (light travel distance)
     * 37.8 Ym – 4 billion light-years – length of the [2374]Huge-LQG
     * 75 Ym – redshift 0.95 – 8 billion light-years – approximate
       distance to the [2375]supernova SN 2002dd in the [2376]Hubble Deep
       Field North (light travel distance)
     * 85 Ym – redshift 1.6 – 9 billion light-years – approximate distance
       to the [2377]gamma-ray burst [2378]GRB 990123 (light travel
       distance)
     * 94.6 Ym – 10 billion light-years – approximate distance to quasar
       OQ172
     * 94.6 Ym – 10 billion light-years – length of the
       [2379]Hercules–Corona Borealis Great Wall, one of the [2380]largest
       and most massive-known cosmic structures known

100 yottameters

   [[2381]edit]

   To help compare different [2382]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [2383]distances starting at 100 [2384]Ym (10^26 [2385]m or 11 billion
   [2386]light-years). At this scale, expansion of the [2387]universe
   becomes significant. Distance of these objects are derived from their
   measured [2388]redshifts, which depend on the [2389]cosmological models
   used.
     * 124 Ym – redshift 7.54 – 13.1 billion light-years – [2390]light
       travel distance (LTD) to the [2391]quasar [2392]ULAS J1342+0928,
       the [2393]most distant-known quasar as of 2017
     * 130 Ym – redshift 1,000 – 13.8 billion light-years – distance (LTD)
       to the source of the [2394]cosmic microwave background radiation;
       radius of the observable [2395]universe measured as a LTD
     * 260 Ym – 27.4 billion light-years – diameter of the observable
       universe (double LTD)
     * 440 Ym – 46 billion light-years – radius of the universe measured
       as a [2396]comoving distance
     * 590 Ym – 62 billion light-years – cosmological [2397]event horizon:
       the largest comoving distance from which light will ever reach us
       (the observer) at any time in the future
     * 886.48 Ym – 93.7 billion light-years – the diameter of the
       [2398]observable universe (twice the [2399]particle horizon);
       however, there might be unobserved distances that are even greater.

1 ronnameter

   [[2400]edit]

   The ronnametre
   ([2401]SI symbol: Rm) is a [2402]unit of [2403]length in the
   [2404]metric system equal to 10^27 [2405]meters.^[2406][56]

   To help compare different [2407]orders of magnitude, this section lists
   [2408]distances starting at 1 Rm (10^27 [2409]m or 105.7 billion
   [2410]light-years). At this scale, expansion of the [2411]universe
   becomes significant. Distance of these objects are derived from their
   measured [2412]redshifts, which depend on the [2413]cosmological models
   used.
     * >1 Rm – >105.7 billion light-years – size of universe beyond the
       [2414]cosmic light horizon, depending on its curvature; if the
       curvature is zero (i.e. the universe is spatially flat), the value
       can be [2415]infinite (see [2416]Shape of the universe) as
       previously mentioned.
     * 2.764 Rm - 292.2 billion light-years – circumference of the
       observable universe, as it is in the shape of a sphere.
     * ≈10^10^10^122[2417]light-years – the possible size of the universe
       after [2418]cosmological inflation.
     * ≈∞ light-years – theoretical size of the [2419]multiverse if it
       exists.
